For the numerical part, put A=\frac14+\omega. When 4(A+\varepsilon)\frac{k}{k-1}\log k<4 we use M_k(L,\tau)\le M_k(L,L)=LM_k\le L\frac{k}{k-1}\log k [15, Cor. 6.4]. The remaining range of \varepsilon, up to A, is cut into intervals of length at most 1/2000; on [e,e'] we take (L,\tau)=(4(A+e'),4(A-e)), which dominates by monotonicity, and certify \sup_\sigma W(\sigma)<4 with rational (a,s_1) exactly as in the proof of Theorem 15. The certificates are code/cert_floor_hybrid_k*_w*.json, written by code/floor_hybrid.py in ball arithmetic (python-flint) and re-checked by the same script with –check in a different interval library (mpmath.iv). ◻

Remark 49 (how sharp). For each \omega in the table the same weight fails at the next k: the floating-point \sup W reaches 4 on some interval of \varepsilon (for example k=27, \omega=0.007, near \varepsilon\approx0.0548). So the table shows the reach of this weight, not of the method; the true floor is presumably much higher (Heuristic): the cap-free quantity of Proposition 24 exceeds 4 at k=45 only by 0.012, so its threshold is probably not far below k=45, by analogy with Remark 22. The theorem ignores the caps entirely, which only shrink the support; a floor that sees the caps is the natural next step. It also does not apply to supports with several bands, where J contains cross terms between bands, nor to the constructions behind 186 and 182, which we have not read in enough detail.

Related results

Lines of work that grow into their own results live in separate papers (index: papers/README.md; PDFs on the project website). Each carries the same labels as this paper.

  • An independent exact verification of the variational step behind the claimed 2026 bounds H_1\le240 and H_1\le236, with a remark on the caps of a 212 draft (papers/verification-note/, version 1.0, frozen). The exact recomputations of Section 5, written up as a short note, with a section saying for every number whether it rests on one program, two independent programs or a probabilistic check: the variational criteria of Stadlmann (240) and Kintali / Song–Yue (236), each by two programs; the Table 3 / Lemma 9.1 cap inconsistency of the 212 draft; and the re-evaluation of the main terms and loss bounds of the 186 companion. None of it verifies the analytic inputs.

  • The parity barrier and twin primes: what is known and what is missing (papers/parity-barrier/, version 0.14). A survey of the obstacle between bounded gaps and twin primes, read in the sources, with some own results. Current main statements:

    • an exact criterion for which close-prime targets a Polymath8b-type (Liouville-pattern) weight can block: the target graph must be bipartite, so the class blocks exactly H_m<H(2m+1), i.e. 6 for m=1 (a heuristic barrier, not a bound; the core is in a 2014 blog post of Tao, credited);

    • ceilings for Polymath8b’s GEH criteria (no \mathrm{DHL}[k,3] for k\le45), the case k=2 exactly at the parity value 2, and for which \varepsilon the k=3 criterion beats 2 (above 2 at \varepsilon=\frac29,\frac14,\frac7{24}; provably below 2 for \varepsilon\ge0.439; Computed, two programs each);

    • Pintz’s parity-sensitive dichotomy redone with Maynard’s weights. It needs only \theta M_k>1, so it holds for admissible 5-tuples unconditionally, and some even d with 0<|d|\le12 has \lambda(p+d)=-1 for infinitely many primes p (Pintz: 16). Proved here, possibly folklore; the ratios are exact and checked by two programs. The obstruction blocks “a prime next to an odd number of prime factors” but not this disjunction;

    • a consistency test against Chen’s theorem: the class does not block “p prime, p+2\in P_2”, while it blocks each half (p+2 prime; p+2 a product of exactly two primes), as the literature says (Lean-checked for the weights);

    • small global Gowers norms (of any fixed order) do not constrain the shift-2 correlation: random block functions with small U^d norms have correlation near \pm1, and a \{0,2\}-valued “fake prime” function with small \|g-1\|_{U^d} has almost no pairs g(n)g(n+2)\ne0 (Proved here, folklore in substance); so the Green–Tao finite-complexity theory cannot see twins; Tao’s localisation for \lambda (an equivalence of open conjectures) relies on multiplicativity at small primes and has no analogue for \Lambda at short scales;

    • averaging over the shift (Matomäki–Radziwiłł–Tao): for almost all h in windows of length X^{8/33+\varepsilon} the Hardy–Littlewood asymptotic is proved, and for \lambda(n)\lambda(n+h) any H\to\infty suffices; a window identity shows that the twin count is a second difference of the second moment of primes in windows of fixed length, and no averaged statement of this kind reaches a single shift (Literature, with our reading);

    • what the Friedlander–Iwaniec and Bombieri asymptotic sieves need for \Lambda(n+2), Heath-Brown’s and Friedlander–Iwaniec’s bilinear structures, Sawin–Shusterman’s \mathbb F_q[T] proof and the step with no integer analogue, the twisted Type I input in the log-averaged regime (conditionally up to level (\log x)^{c_0}), and the Siegel-zero world;

    • several small corrections to the literature (Polymath8b Remark 8.1 and Proposition 6.5, a constant in a 2019 preprint).

    It makes no claim of progress on the twin prime conjecture, which is open.

Programme

Track A: lowering the bound

  1. Climb the ladder. The certified pipeline now stands at k=50 (H_1\le246, the established bound, via the \varepsilon-enlarged simplex). Stadlmann’s region (k=49, 240): we reproduce the variational step exactly (Theorem 28, Corollary 29), so the project’s bound is 240 conditionally on the unverified reduction [19, Prop. 1] and equidistribution estimates of [19]. For 212 (k=45, [3]) we confirm the variational criterion for the caps of their Table 3 (Proposition 31); before the project’s bound can move, the discrepancy between their Table 3 and Lemma 9.1 must be resolved; we have the tool for optimising on the capped support (Remark 33), and for the caps of Lemma 9.1 no polynomial in our 846-dimensional space reaches 3.99 (Proposition 35, resting on one program’s Gram weights and a randomised check), which suggests that the draft’s value belongs to its Table 3 caps. For 236 (k=48) the variational criterion of [8] holds with our own degree-22 vector (Proposition 37), and the capped numerator of the published witness of [18] is reproduced exactly, integer for integer, by two independent programs (Proposition 38), so the project’s bound is 236 conditionally (Corollary 39); the analytic input remains Claimed. Our own vector is confirmed by two programs; Proposition 41 makes such checks cheaper when only the inequality is needed. Then 186 and 182.

  2. Floor of the method (our main original direction). For the plain simplex the dual bound M_k\le\frac{k}{k-1}\log k shows where the method stops (k\ge51). For the \varepsilon-enlarged simplex Theorem 15 gives k\ge30, which is still far from the expected truth near 50; Section 4 explains what a sharper weight must do, and Remark 21 indicates (Heuristic) that it must depend on more of t than t_m and S (a Collatz–Wielandt weight \psi_m=\int F\,dt_m/F built from a good positive test function F is the natural candidate). For the one-band hybrid supports of [19, 3], Theorem 48 gives the first such bound we know of (k\ge29 at Stadlmann’s level, k\ge27 at that of [3]), still far from 49 and 45; it ignores the caps. A rigorous dual certificate there would say how far one-band supports can go with the present inputs, and which input (which Type I/II/III range) is binding; the constructions behind 186 and 182 are of a different kind and would need their own dual bound.

  3. Which rung is cheap. From Table 1, the gain per unit of k below the claimed frontier is 182\to176\to168\to162\to158 for k=39,\dots,35: the step 38\to37 (gain 8) is worth the most; 39\to38 and 37\to36 give 6 each.

Problem 50 (Open; partly answered by Theorem 48 for one band, without using the caps). Give a rigorous upper bound for the variational quantity of [19] as a function of k and of the admissible exponents, and deduce the least k the hybrid method can reach with the currently proved estimates.

Track B: the parity barrier

No sieve of the above kind can distinguish integers with an even number of prime factors from those with an odd number. Known ways around the barrier all inject bilinear or structural information. We will study, one per session, what each would need in order to say something about twin primes: Chen’s theorem [4]; Bombieri’s asymptotic sieve [2]; the parity-breaking sieve of Friedlander and Iwaniec [6]; Heath-Brown’s theorem that Siegel zeros would imply the conjecture [7]; the function-field proof of Sawin and Shusterman [17]; and the entropy-based results on correlations of multiplicative functions [20].

Problem 51 (Open, to be made precise in Lean). State, as a single explicit hypothesis on bilinear sums over n(n+2), the weakest known input that implies the twin prime conjecture, and formalise the implication.

Rules of this paper

A bound is listed as reproduced by this project only if its variational step has an exact certificate (for the 2026 bounds, confirmed by two independent programs) and its combinatorial step is Lean-checked; every unverified analytic input is named next to it. Ideas are logged with their outcome, including failures. Nothing here is peer reviewed.

History of this document

Versions 0.1–0.18 (12–15 November 2026) carried a long abstract that recorded each step as it was made; it is preserved in the repository’s git history (paper/main.tex at commit 66c97f2) and, session by session, in the project log (log/research-log.md). Its content is in the body of this paper and in the verification note.

Later versions, one item each:

The 182 claim remains unverified (restoration losses and analytic inputs unchecked). Related results: the parity paper’s version 0.3 (the twisted Type I input at level (\log x)^{c_0}, conditional on a preprint), and its version 0.4 (Heath-Brown’s x^3+2y^3, read in the source).

  2. E. Bombieri, The asymptotic sieve, 1976.

  3. F. Charton, L. Hong, K. Lau, K. Ono, G. Remy, H. C. Siu, A. A. Swaminathan, J. Thorner, Y. Xie, A new bound for small gaps between primes, preliminary draft, 3 September 2026. https://primegaps.axiommath.ai/bgp212.pdf

  4. J. R. Chen, On the representation of a larger even integer as the sum of a prime and the product of at most two primes, 1973.

  8. S. Kintali, Improved bounded gaps between primes, preprint, 1 September 2026. https://shivakintali.github.io/papers/Prime-Gaps.pdf

  9. L. Lovász, Normal hypergraphs and the perfect graph conjecture, Discrete Math., 1972.

  10. J. Maynard, Small gaps between primes, Ann. of Math. 181 (2015), 383–413.

  13. OpenAI, Improved short gaps between primes, preprint dated 30 August 2026, with the repository openai/PrimeGaps186.

  17. W. Sawin, M. Shusterman, On the Chowla and twin primes conjectures over \mathbb F_q[T], Ann. of Math., 2022.
